I… I came in because I’m not sleeping right. I’ve always been very disciplined about sleep—early meetings, travel schedules, all of that, you learn to protect it. I used to tell my team sleep was a performance issue, which sounds very corporate, I know.

But since my cousin died, I lie awake replaying that night. And then if I do sleep, I’m waking up at 3 or 4 in the morning with these little scraps of hospital images in my head. Then I’m in board meetings feeling like I’ve slept in pieces, not a real night. It’s starting to scare me a bit, honestly.

Most of the time… not exactly in every setting. If I’m in my office with the door closed, I can usually function. But when I’m driving, it’s like my whole body goes on alert. I’m checking mirrors constantly, watching how fast the trucks are coming up beside me, thinking about where I’d pull off if I had to.

It’s ridiculous, because I know the route so well—I used to take calls on that drive, plan my day, all of that—but now I’m scanning everything. So yes, I suppose anxious, but it feels more like being braced all the time.

It’s starting to scare me a bit, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know. That’s a hard word. I’ve managed companies through crises, and I’m used to thinking in terms of responsibility and what could have been done differently, but this feels—less clean than that.\n\nI keep turning pieces of it over in my head, but I’m not sure I’d call it guilt exactly. Maybe regret, or just… wishing something had gone differently. I’m sorry, I know that’s not a very direct answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time… not exactly in every setting. If I’m in my office with the door closed, I can usually function. But when I’m driving, it’s like my whole body goes on alert. I’m checking mirrors constantly, watching how fast the trucks are coming up beside me, thinking about where I’d pull off if I had to.\n\nEven on the same road I’ve taken to the office for years, I feel tight, like I’m waiting for something to happen. It’s ridiculous, because I know the route so well—I used to take calls on that drive, plan my day, all of that—but now I’m scanning everything. So yes, I suppose anxious, but it feels more like being braced all the time.\n\nDoctor: Are there things or situations you've been avoiding since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve thought about it that way. Avoiding… maybe not consciously. I’m still going to work, still taking meetings, still doing the normal obligations. I’ve spent so many years being the person who shows up no matter what, you know, flights at dawn, dinners with investors, the whole machinery of it.\n\nBut if you mean specific situations, I… I don’t know. I haven’t made a list in my head. There are things I hesitate around, maybe, but I’m not sure I’d call it avoidance. Not like I’m literally back there, exactly. I know where I am. But there are these moments—usually very ordinary ones, which is what makes it so strange—when something just cuts through.

A phone ringing late, for instance. I used to be very good about late calls, because in my line of work you learn to take calls at all hours, different time zones and all that. But now if the phone rings at night, I suddenly get this sharp picture of the hospital corridor, the lights, that feeling of standing there waiting.

Or on the highway, if headlights glare in a certain way, I’ll hear the police officer’s voice in my head again. Not a memory I’m choosing to think about—it just arrives. I have to stop for a second, steady myself, remind myself I’m here. So… yes, in that sense.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know that I can name anything cleanly. I mean, I haven’t stopped going to work or anything like that. I still go where I’m supposed to go.

There are moments where I’ll choose a different route or delay leaving, but then I tell myself it’s because of traffic or because I need to make one more call first. It’s hard to know whether that counts. I’ve spent my whole career optimizing schedules, you know, so it’s very easy for me to make something sound practical when maybe it’s… not entirely practical.

As for specific places or activities, I’m not sure. Nothing jumps out as an obvious “I refuse to go there” sort of thing.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve sat down and decided, “I won’t talk about it.” People don’t bring it up much, honestly. In my world, everyone is very skilled at behaving as though difficult things are logistical complications—send flowers, adjust the calendar, say the appropriate thing, move on.

If someone asks, I usually give a brief answer. I suppose I do keep it… contained. But I don’t know if that’s avoidance or just privacy. I’ve always been private. Even when my father was ill years ago, I remember I still chaired a board call from the hallway, which sounds terrible now, but at the time it felt like being competent.

Sorry, I’m drifting. Conversations about my cousin’s death—I can’t really say I’ve been clearly avoiding them. I just don’t tend to go into it unless someone asks directly.

I don’t think I’ve sat down and decided, “I won’t talk about it.” People don’t bring it up much, honestly. In my world, everyone is very skilled at behaving as though difficult things are logistical complications—send flowers, adjust the calendar, say the appropriate thing, move on.\n\nIf someone asks, I usually give a brief answer. I suppose I do keep it… contained. But I don’t know if that’s avoidance or just privacy. I’ve always been private. Even when my father was ill years ago, I remember I still chaired a board call from the hallway, which sounds terrible now, but at the time it felt like being competent.\n\nSorry, I’m drifting. Conversations about my cousin’s death—I can’t really say I’ve been clearly avoiding them. I just don’t tend to go into it unless someone asks directly.\n\n[Last Question]\nHas your ability to function at work or in your daily life been significantly impacted since your cousin's death?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 58 / chief_executive / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I’m still showing up for the most essential meetings, but I’ve had to delegate community obligations and shorten my workdays because I’m not as steady or effective with people as I normally am.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… though I keep wanting to qualify that. I’m still showing up for the essential things—the meetings that really can’t be moved, the decisions people need from me. I’m not just disappearing.

But I’m not functioning at my usual level. I’ve started handing off more of the community obligations, the receptions and committees and those sorts of things. Normally I can do that kind of room easily—remember everyone’s names, make the right comment, keep things moving. Lately I just don’t feel steady enough with people.

And I’ve been shortening some workdays, which I almost never do. I tell myself I’m being efficient, but the truth is I’m not as effective by the end of the day as I should be. So yes, it has had an impact. Not catastrophic, but noticeable.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I… I came in because I’m not sleeping right. I’ve always been very disciplined about sleep—early meetings, travel schedules, all of that, you learn to protect it. I used to tell my team sleep was a performance issue, which sounds very corporate, I know.\n\nBut since my cousin died, I lie awake replaying that night. And then if I do sleep, I’m waking up at 3 or 4 in the morning with these little scraps of hospital images in my head. Then I’m in board meetings feeling like I’ve slept in pieces, not a real night. It’s starting to scare me a bit, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know. That’s a hard word. I’ve managed companies through crises, and I’m used to thinking in terms of responsibility and what could have been done differently, but this feels—less clean than that.\n\nI keep turning pieces of it over in my head, but I’m not sure I’d call it guilt exactly. Maybe regret, or just… wishing something had gone differently. I’m sorry, I know that’s not a very direct answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time… not exactly in every setting. If I’m in my office with the door closed, I can usually function. But when I’m driving, it’s like my whole body goes on alert. I’m checking mirrors constantly, watching how fast the trucks are coming up beside me, thinking about where I’d pull off if I had to.\n\nEven on the same road I’ve taken to the office for years, I feel tight, like I’m waiting for something to happen. It’s ridiculous, because I know the route so well—I used to take calls on that drive, plan my day, all of that—but now I’m scanning everything. So yes, I suppose anxious, but it feels more like being braced all the time.\n\nDoctor: Are there things or situations you've been avoiding since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve thought about it that way. Avoiding… maybe not consciously. I’m still going to work, still taking meetings, still doing the normal obligations. I’ve spent so many years being the person who shows up no matter what, you know, flights at dawn, dinners with investors, the whole machinery of it.\n\nBut if you mean specific situations, I… I don’t know. I haven’t made a list in my head. There are things I hesitate around, maybe, but I’m not sure I’d call it avoidance. My assistant did ask if I wanted to move a meeting the other day, and I gave this long explanation about traffic patterns and timing, which probably sounded absurd.\n\nSorry—what exactly do you mean by avoiding? It’s hard to know whether that counts. I’ve spent my whole career optimizing schedules, you know, so it’s very easy for me to make something sound practical when maybe it’s… not entirely practical.\n\nAs for specific places or activities, I’m not sure. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Sad, yes, but it’s a little stranger than that. I don’t know that I would say I’m crying all day or anything so clear.

At the prayer gatherings, for example, I knew exactly how to behave—stand where I should, thank people, nod, say the right things. And my nieces were very sweet, trying to comfort me. Normally that would reach me, you know? I’m usually… not sentimental exactly, but I can feel affection when it’s there.

Lately it’s like I’m doing the proper motions, but there’s no warmth coming back from inside me. No relief. Almost like that part of me has gone quiet. So yes, empty is probably closer than just sad.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.0, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I… I came in because I’m not sleeping right. I’ve always been very disciplined about sleep—early meetings, travel schedules, all of that, you learn to protect it. I used to tell my team sleep was a performance issue, which sounds very corporate, I know.\n\nBut since my cousin died, I lie awake replaying that night. And then if I do sleep, I’m waking up at 3 or 4 in the morning with these little scraps of hospital images in my head. Then I’m in board meetings feeling like I’ve slept in pieces, not a real night. It’s starting to scare me a bit, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know. That’s a hard word. I’ve managed companies through crises, and I’m used to thinking in terms of responsibility and what could have been done differently, but this feels—less clean than that.\n\nI keep turning pieces of it over in my head, but I’m not sure I’d call it guilt exactly. Maybe regret, or just… wishing something had gone differently. I’m sorry, I know that’s not a very direct answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time… not exactly in every setting. If I’m in my office with the door closed, I can usually function. But when I’m driving, it’s like my whole body goes on alert. I’m checking mirrors constantly, watching how fast the trucks are coming up beside me, thinking about where I’d pull off if I had to.\n\nEven on the same road I’ve taken to the office for years, I feel tight, like I’m waiting for something to happen. It’s ridiculous, because I know the route so well—I used to take calls on that drive, plan my day, all of that—but now I’m scanning everything. So yes, I suppose anxious, but it feels more like being braced all the time.\n\nDoctor: Are there things or situations you've been avoiding since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve thought about it that way. Avoiding… maybe not consciously. I’m still going to work, still taking meetings, still doing the normal obligations. I’ve spent so many years being the person who shows up no matter what, you know, flights at dawn, dinners with investors, the whole machinery of it.\n\nBut if you mean specific situations, I… I don’t know. I haven’t made a list in my head. There are things I hesitate around, maybe, but I’m not sure I’d call it avoidance. My assistant did ask if I wanted to move a meeting the other day, and I gave this long explanation about traffic patterns and timing, which probably sounded absurd.\n\nSorry—what exactly do you mean by avoiding? Like places, or conversations, or…?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever experience memories of that night that feel like they're happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Not like I’m literally back there, exactly. I know where I am. But there are these moments—usually very ordinary ones, which is what makes it so strange—when something just cuts through.\n\nA phone ringing late, for instance. I used to be very good about late calls, because in my line of work you learn to take calls at all hours, different time zones and all that. But now if the phone rings at night, I suddenly get this sharp picture of the hospital corridor, the lights, that feeling of standing there waiting.\n\nOr on the highway, if headlights glare in a certain way, I’ll hear the police officer’s voice in my head again. Not a memory I’m choosing to think about—it just arrives. I have to stop for a second, steady myself, remind myself I’m here. So… yes, in that sense.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific places or activities that you've been avoiding since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can name anything cleanly. I mean, I haven’t stopped going to work or anything like that. I still go where I’m supposed to go.\n\nThere are moments where I’ll choose a different route or delay leaving, but then I tell myself it’s because of traffic or because I need to make one more call first. It’s hard to know whether that counts. I’ve spent my whole career optimizing schedules, you know, so it’s very easy for me to make something sound practical when maybe it’s… not entirely practical.\n\nAs for specific places or activities, I’m not sure. Nothing jumps out as an obvious “I refuse to go there” sort of thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding conversations about your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve sat down and decided, “I won’t talk about it.” People don’t bring it up much, honestly. In my world, everyone is very skilled at behaving as though difficult things are logistical complications—send flowers, adjust the calendar, say the appropriate thing, move on.\n\nIf someone asks, I usually give a brief answer. I suppose I do keep it… contained. But I don’t know if that’s avoidance or just privacy. I’ve always been private. Even when my father was ill years ago, I remember I still chaired a board call from the hallway, which sounds terrible now, but at the time it felt like being competent.\n\nSorry, I’m drifting. Conversations about my cousin’s death—I can’t really say I’ve been clearly avoiding them. I just don’t tend to go into it unless someone asks directly.\n\nDoctor: Has your ability to function at work or in your daily life been significantly impacted since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… though I keep wanting to qualify that. I’m still showing up for the essential things—the meetings that really can’t be moved, the decisions people need from me. I’m not just disappearing.\n\nBut I’m not functioning at my usual level. I’ve started handing off more of the community obligations, the receptions and committees and those sorts of things. Normally I can do that kind of room easily—remember everyone’s names, make the right comment, keep things moving. Lately I just don’t feel steady enough with people.\n\nAnd I’ve been shortening some workdays, which I almost never do. I tell myself I’m being efficient, but the truth is I’m not as effective by the end of the day as I should be. So yes, it has had an impact. Not catastrophic, but noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or empty most of the time since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Sad, yes, but it’s a little stranger than that. I don’t know that I would say I’m crying all day or anything so clear.\n\nAt the prayer gatherings, for example, I knew exactly how to behave—stand where I should, thank people, nod, say the right things. And my nieces were very sweet, trying to comfort me. Normally that would reach me, you know? I’m usually… not sentimental exactly, but I can feel affection when it’s there.\n\nLately it’s like I’m doing the proper motions, but there’s no warmth coming back from inside me. No relief. So yes, empty is probably closer than just sad.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you lost interest in things you used to enjoy since your cousin's death?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 58 / chief_executive / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know that I’d call it a loss of interest exactly. I mean, I haven’t been going out of my way to do pleasant things, but then I’ve never been someone with a long list of hobbies. Work has always taken up most of the oxygen in the room.

There are things I usually enjoy—hosting, certain dinners, being involved in events—but lately I’ve treated them more like obligations to be managed. Though some of that may just be timing. There’s always some luncheon or foundation thing, and honestly half the time those are more about who gets seated next to whom than enjoyment.

So… I’m not sure. I’ve noticed I’m less drawn to those things, maybe, but I couldn’t say clearly that I’ve lost interest.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I… I came in because I’m not sleeping right. I’ve always been very disciplined about sleep—early meetings, travel schedules, all of that, you learn to protect it. I used to tell my team sleep was a performance issue, which sounds very corporate, I know.\n\nBut since my cousin died, I lie awake replaying that night. And then if I do sleep, I’m waking up at 3 or 4 in the morning with these little scraps of hospital images in my head. Then I’m in board meetings feeling like I’ve slept in pieces, not a real night. It’s starting to scare me a bit, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know. That’s a hard word. I’ve managed companies through crises, and I’m used to thinking in terms of responsibility and what could have been done differently, but this feels—less clean than that.\n\nI keep turning pieces of it over in my head, but I’m not sure I’d call it guilt exactly. Maybe regret, or just… wishing something had gone differently. I’m sorry, I know that’s not a very direct answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time… not exactly in every setting. If I’m in my office with the door closed, I can usually function. But when I’m driving, it’s like my whole body goes on alert. I’m checking mirrors constantly, watching how fast the trucks are coming up beside me, thinking about where I’d pull off if I had to.\n\nEven on the same road I’ve taken to the office for years, I feel tight, like I’m waiting for something to happen. It’s ridiculous, because I know the route so well—I used to take calls on that drive, plan my day, all of that—but now I’m scanning everything. So yes, I suppose anxious, but it feels more like being braced all the time.\n\nDoctor: Are there things or situations you've been avoiding since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve thought about it that way. Avoiding… maybe not consciously. I’m still going to work, still taking meetings, still doing the normal obligations. I’ve spent so many years being the person who shows up no matter what, you know, flights at dawn, dinners with investors, the whole machinery of it.\n\nBut if you mean specific situations, I… I don’t know. I haven’t made a list in my head. There are things I hesitate around, maybe, but I’m not sure I’d call it avoidance. My assistant did ask if I wanted to move a meeting the other day, and I gave this long explanation about traffic patterns and timing, which probably sounded absurd.\n\nSorry—what exactly do you mean by avoiding? Like places, or conversations, or…?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever experience memories of that night that feel like they're happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Not like I’m literally back there, exactly. I know where I am. But there are these moments—usually very ordinary ones, which is what makes it so strange—when something just cuts through.\n\nA phone ringing late, for instance. I used to be very good about late calls, because in my line of work you learn to take calls at all hours, different time zones and all that. But now if the phone rings at night, I suddenly get this sharp picture of the hospital corridor, the lights, that feeling of standing there waiting.\n\nOr on the highway, if headlights glare in a certain way, I’ll hear the police officer’s voice in my head again. Not a memory I’m choosing to think about—it just arrives. I have to stop for a second, steady myself, remind myself I’m here. So… yes, in that sense.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific places or activities that you've been avoiding since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can name anything cleanly. I mean, I haven’t stopped going to work or anything like that. I still go where I’m supposed to go.\n\nThere are moments where I’ll choose a different route or delay leaving, but then I tell myself it’s because of traffic or because I need to make one more call first. It’s hard to know whether that counts. I’ve spent my whole career optimizing schedules, you know, so it’s very easy for me to make something sound practical when maybe it’s… not entirely practical.\n\nAs for specific places or activities, I’m not sure. Nothing jumps out as an obvious “I refuse to go there” sort of thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding conversations about your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve sat down and decided, “I won’t talk about it.” People don’t bring it up much, honestly. In my world, everyone is very skilled at behaving as though difficult things are logistical complications—send flowers, adjust the calendar, say the appropriate thing, move on.\n\nIf someone asks, I usually give a brief answer. I suppose I do keep it… contained. But I don’t know if that’s avoidance or just privacy. I’ve always been private. Even when my father was ill years ago, I remember I still chaired a board call from the hallway, which sounds terrible now, but at the time it felt like being competent.\n\nSorry, I’m drifting. Conversations about my cousin’s death—I can’t really say I’ve been clearly avoiding them. I just don’t tend to go into it unless someone asks directly.\n\nDoctor: Has your ability to function at work or in your daily life been significantly impacted since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… though I keep wanting to qualify that. I’m still showing up for the essential things—the meetings that really can’t be moved, the decisions people need from me. I’m not just disappearing.\n\nBut I’m not functioning at my usual level. I’ve started handing off more of the community obligations, the receptions and committees and those sorts of things. Normally I can do that kind of room easily—remember everyone’s names, make the right comment, keep things moving. Lately I just don’t feel steady enough with people.\n\nAnd I’ve been shortening some workdays, which I almost never do. I tell myself I’m being efficient, but the truth is I’m not as effective by the end of the day as I should be. So yes, it has had an impact. Not catastrophic, but noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or empty most of the time since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Sad, yes, but it’s a little stranger than that. I don’t know that I would say I’m crying all day or anything so clear.\n\nAt the prayer gatherings, for example, I knew exactly how to behave—stand where I should, thank people, nod, say the right things. And my nieces were very sweet, trying to comfort me. Normally that would reach me, you know? I’m usually… not sentimental exactly, but I can feel affection when it’s there.\n\nLately it’s like I’m doing the proper motions, but there’s no warmth coming back from inside me. No relief. Almost like that part of me has gone quiet. So yes, empty is probably closer than just sad.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it a loss of interest exactly. I mean, I haven’t been going out of my way to do pleasant things, but then I’ve never been someone with a long list of hobbies. Work has always taken up most of the oxygen in the room.\n\nThere are things I usually enjoy—hosting, certain dinners, being involved in events—but lately I’ve treated them more like obligations to be managed. Though some of that may just be timing. There’s always some luncheon or foundation thing, and honestly half the time those are more about who gets seated next to whom than enjoyment.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I’ve noticed I’m less drawn to those things, maybe, but I couldn’t say clearly that I’ve lost interest.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. And my nieces were very sweet, trying to comfort me. Normally that would reach me, you know? I’m usually… not sentimental exactly, but I can feel affection when it’s there.\n\nLately it’s like I’m doing the proper motions, but there’s no warmth coming back from inside me. No relief. Almost like that part of me has gone quiet. So yes, empty is probably closer than just sad.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it a loss of interest exactly. I mean, I haven’t been going out of my way to do pleasant things, but then I’ve never been someone with a long list of hobbies. Work has always taken up most of the oxygen in the room.\n\nThere are things I usually enjoy—hosting, certain dinners, being involved in events—but lately I’ve treated them more like obligations to be managed. Though some of that may just be timing. There’s always some luncheon or foundation thing, and honestly half the time those are more about who gets seated next to whom than enjoyment.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I’ve noticed I’m less drawn to those things, maybe, but I couldn’t say clearly that I’ve lost interest.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed yourself pulling back from activities that you normally find enjoyable or fulfilling since your cousin's death?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 58 / chief_executive / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I’m still showing up for the most essential meetings, but I’ve had to delegate community obligations and shorten my workdays because I’m not as steady or effective with people as I normally am.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I suppose I have. I’m still going to the things that really matter—if there’s a meeting where people genuinely need me, I’m there. I’m not hiding in my bedroom or anything.

But the other pieces… the community events, the receptions, the committee things—I’ve been passing more of those off. Which is unusual for me. I used to be able to walk into a room and know exactly how to manage it. Who needs a word, who’s offended by what seating arrangement, all of that nonsense.

Lately I just don’t feel as steady with people. And I’ve shortened a few workdays too, which I hate admitting. By the end of the day I’m just not as effective as I normally am. So yes, I’ve pulled back some, even if I keep telling myself it’s only from the nonessential things.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I… I came in because I’m not sleeping right. I’ve always been very disciplined about sleep—early meetings, travel schedules, all of that, you learn to protect it. I used to tell my team sleep was a performance issue, which sounds very corporate, I know.\n\nBut since my cousin died, I lie awake replaying that night. And then if I do sleep, I’m waking up at 3 or 4 in the morning with these little scraps of hospital images in my head. Then I’m in board meetings feeling like I’ve slept in pieces, not a real night. It’s starting to scare me a bit, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know. That’s a hard word. I’ve managed companies through crises, and I’m used to thinking in terms of responsibility and what could have been done differently, but this feels—less clean than that.\n\nI keep turning pieces of it over in my head, but I’m not sure I’d call it guilt exactly. Maybe regret, or just… wishing something had gone differently. I’m sorry, I know that’s not a very direct answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time… not exactly in every setting. If I’m in my office with the door closed, I can usually function. But when I’m driving, it’s like my whole body goes on alert. I’m checking mirrors constantly, watching how fast the trucks are coming up beside me, thinking about where I’d pull off if I had to.\n\nEven on the same road I’ve taken to the office for years, I feel tight, like I’m waiting for something to happen. It’s ridiculous, because I know the route so well—I used to take calls on that drive, plan my day, all of that—but now I’m scanning everything. So yes, I suppose anxious, but it feels more like being braced all the time.\n\nDoctor: Are there things or situations you've been avoiding since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve thought about it that way. Avoiding… maybe not consciously. I’m still going to work, still taking meetings, still doing the normal obligations. I’ve spent so many years being the person who shows up no matter what, you know, flights at dawn, dinners with investors, the whole machinery of it.\n\nBut if you mean specific situations, I… I don’t know. I haven’t made a list in my head. There are things I hesitate around, maybe, but I’m not sure I’d call it avoidance. My assistant did ask if I wanted to move a meeting the other day, and I gave this long explanation about traffic patterns and timing, which probably sounded absurd.\n\nSorry—what exactly do you mean by avoiding? Like places, or conversations, or…?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever experience memories of that night that feel like they're happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Not like I’m literally back there, exactly. I know where I am. But there are these moments—usually very ordinary ones, which is what makes it so strange—when something just cuts through.\n\nA phone ringing late, for instance. I used to be very good about late calls, because in my line of work you learn to take calls at all hours, different time zones and all that. But now if the phone rings at night, I suddenly get this sharp picture of the hospital corridor, the lights, that feeling of standing there waiting.\n\nOr on the highway, if headlights glare in a certain way, I’ll hear the police officer’s voice in my head again. Not a memory I’m choosing to think about—it just arrives. I have to stop for a second, steady myself, remind myself I’m here. So… yes, in that sense.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific places or activities that you've been avoiding since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can name anything cleanly. I mean, I haven’t stopped going to work or anything like that. I still go where I’m supposed to go.\n\nThere are moments where I’ll choose a different route or delay leaving, but then I tell myself it’s because of traffic or because I need to make one more call first. It’s hard to know whether that counts. I’ve spent my whole career optimizing schedules, you know, so it’s very easy for me to make something sound practical when maybe it’s… not entirely practical.\n\nAs for specific places or activities, I’m not sure. Nothing jumps out as an obvious “I refuse to go there” sort of thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding conversations about your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve sat down and decided, “I won’t talk about it.” People don’t bring it up much, honestly. In my world, everyone is very skilled at behaving as though difficult things are logistical complications—send flowers, adjust the calendar, say the appropriate thing, move on.\n\nIf someone asks, I usually give a brief answer. I suppose I do keep it… contained. But I don’t know if that’s avoidance or just privacy. I’ve always been private. Even when my father was ill years ago, I remember I still chaired a board call from the hallway, which sounds terrible now, but at the time it felt like being competent.\n\nSorry, I’m drifting. Conversations about my cousin’s death—I can’t really say I’ve been clearly avoiding them. I just don’t tend to go into it unless someone asks directly.\n\nDoctor: Has your ability to function at work or in your daily life been significantly impacted since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… though I keep wanting to qualify that. I’m still showing up for the essential things—the meetings that really can’t be moved, the decisions people need from me. I’m not just disappearing.\n\nBut I’m not functioning at my usual level. I’ve started handing off more of the community obligations, the receptions and committees and those sorts of things. Normally I can do that kind of room easily—remember everyone’s names, make the right comment, keep things moving. Lately I just don’t feel steady enough with people.\n\nAnd I’ve been shortening some workdays, which I almost never do. I tell myself I’m being efficient, but the truth is I’m not as effective by the end of the day as I should be. So yes, it has had an impact. Not catastrophic, but noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or empty most of the time since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Sad, yes, but it’s a little stranger than that. Who needs a word, who’s offended by what seating arrangement, all of that nonsense.\n\nLately I just don’t feel as steady with people. And I’ve shortened a few workdays too, which I hate admitting. By the end of the day I’m just not as effective as I normally am. So yes, I’ve pulled back some, even if I keep telling myself it’s only from the nonessential things.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre there specific things that remind you of your cousin's death that you try to avoid thinking about or looking at?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

I’m not sure I can point to specific things, no. I mean, people keep asking about photographs or places, but it isn’t as tidy as that for me.

There are moments when something feels… too close, I suppose, but then I’ll get distracted by something completely ordinary, like whether the caterer put the coffee service in the wrong room at a gathering—which, honestly, happens more often than it should—and then I realize I haven’t really answered the question.

So no, I don’t think there are particular things I’m deliberately avoiding looking at or thinking about. Not that I’ve noticed clearly.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I… I came in because I’m not sleeping right. I’ve always been very disciplined about sleep—early meetings, travel schedules, all of that, you learn to protect it. I used to tell my team sleep was a performance issue, which sounds very corporate, I know.\n\nBut since my cousin died, I lie awake replaying that night. And then if I do sleep, I’m waking up at 3 or 4 in the morning with these little scraps of hospital images in my head. Then I’m in board meetings feeling like I’ve slept in pieces, not a real night. It’s starting to scare me a bit, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know. That’s a hard word. I’ve managed companies through crises, and I’m used to thinking in terms of responsibility and what could have been done differently, but this feels—less clean than that.\n\nI keep turning pieces of it over in my head, but I’m not sure I’d call it guilt exactly. Maybe regret, or just… wishing something had gone differently. I’m sorry, I know that’s not a very direct answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time… not exactly in every setting. If I’m in my office with the door closed, I can usually function. But when I’m driving, it’s like my whole body goes on alert. I’m checking mirrors constantly, watching how fast the trucks are coming up beside me, thinking about where I’d pull off if I had to.\n\nEven on the same road I’ve taken to the office for years, I feel tight, like I’m waiting for something to happen. It’s ridiculous, because I know the route so well—I used to take calls on that drive, plan my day, all of that—but now I’m scanning everything. So yes, I suppose anxious, but it feels more like being braced all the time.\n\nDoctor: Are there things or situations you've been avoiding since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve thought about it that way. Avoiding… maybe not consciously. I’m still going to work, still taking meetings, still doing the normal obligations. I’ve spent so many years being the person who shows up no matter what, you know, flights at dawn, dinners with investors, the whole machinery of it.\n\nBut if you mean specific situations, I… I don’t know. I haven’t made a list in my head. There are things I hesitate around, maybe, but I’m not sure I’d call it avoidance. My assistant did ask if I wanted to move a meeting the other day, and I gave this long explanation about traffic patterns and timing, which probably sounded absurd.\n\nSorry—what exactly do you mean by avoiding? Like places, or conversations, or…?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever experience memories of that night that feel like they're happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Not like I’m literally back there, exactly. I used to be very good about late calls, because in my line of work you learn to take calls at all hours, different time zones and all that. But now if the phone rings at night, I suddenly get this sharp picture of the hospital corridor, the lights, that feeling of standing there waiting.\n\nOr on the highway, if headlights glare in a certain way, I’ll hear the police officer’s voice in my head again. Not a memory I’m choosing to think about—it just arrives. I have to stop for a second, steady myself, remind myself I’m here. So… yes, in that sense.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific places or activities that you've been avoiding since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can name anything cleanly. I mean, I haven’t stopped going to work or anything like that. I still go where I’m supposed to go.\n\nThere are moments where I’ll choose a different route or delay leaving, but then I tell myself it’s because of traffic or because I need to make one more call first. It’s hard to know whether that counts. I’ve spent my whole career optimizing schedules, you know, so it’s very easy for me to make something sound practical when maybe it’s… not entirely practical.\n\nAs for specific places or activities, I’m not sure. Nothing jumps out as an obvious “I refuse to go there” sort of thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding conversations about your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve sat down and decided, “I won’t talk about it.” People don’t bring it up much, honestly. In my world, everyone is very skilled at behaving as though difficult things are logistical complications—send flowers, adjust the calendar, say the appropriate thing, move on.\n\nIf someone asks, I usually give a brief answer. I suppose I do keep it… contained. But I don’t know if that’s avoidance or just privacy. I’ve always been private. Even when my father was ill years ago, I remember I still chaired a board call from the hallway, which sounds terrible now, but at the time it felt like being competent.\n\nSorry, I’m drifting. Conversations about my cousin’s death—I can’t really say I’ve been clearly avoiding them. I just don’t tend to go into it unless someone asks directly.\n\nDoctor: Has your ability to function at work or in your daily life been significantly impacted since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… though I keep wanting to qualify that. I’m still showing up for the essential things—the meetings that really can’t be moved, the decisions people need from me. I’m not just disappearing.\n\nBut I’m not functioning at my usual level. I’ve started handing off more of the community obligations, the receptions and committees and those sorts of things. Normally I can do that kind of room easily—remember everyone’s names, make the right comment, keep things moving. Lately I just don’t feel steady enough with people.\n\nAnd I’ve been shortening some workdays, which I almost never do. I tell myself I’m being efficient, but the truth is I’m not as effective by the end of the day as I should be. So yes, it has had an impact. Not catastrophic, but noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or empty most of the time since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Sad, yes, but it’s a little stranger than that. pause to steady myself.\n- key: S071\n  name: Trauma_Related_Amnesia\n  description: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).\n  manifestation: What frightens me is that I can account for the funeral arrangements and the calls I made afterward, but parts of the police call and what exactly happened when I reached the hospital are simply blank, which is not like me at all.\n- key: S076\n  name: Inability_Positive_Emotions\n  description: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).\n  manifestation: During the prayer gatherings and even when my nieces tried to comfort me, I found behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.\n  manifestation: I have snapped at my leadership team over small delays and wording in emails, using a tone I would normally consider unacceptable, and then I feel embarrassed because they have not really done anything wrong.\n- key: S079\n  name: Hypervigilance\n  description: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.\n  manifestation: When I drive now, I keep checking the mirrors, the speed of trucks around me, and where I could pull off, and even on familiar roads to the office my body stays tight as if I’m waiting for something to happen.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I’m still showing up for the most essential meetings, but I’ve had to delegate community obligations and shorten my workdays because I’m not as steady or effective with people as I normally am.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 3 weeks\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about three weeks, since the night my cousin died in the highway collision.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 58 / chief_executive / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S027\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it a loss of interest exactly. I mean, I haven’t been going out of my way to do pleasant things, but then I’ve never been someone with a long list of hobbies. Work has always taken up most of the oxygen in the room.\n\nThere are things I usually enjoy—hosting, certain dinners, being involved in events—but lately I’ve treated them more like obligations to be managed. Though some of that may just be timing. There’s always some luncheon or foundation thing, and honestly half the time those are more about who gets seated next to whom than enjoyment.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I’ve noticed I’m less drawn to those things, maybe, but I couldn’t say clearly that I’ve lost interest.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself pulling back from activities that you normally find enjoyable or fulfilling since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose I have. I’m still going to the things that really matter—if there’s a meeting where people genuinely need me, I’m there. I’m not hiding in my bedroom or anything.\n\nBut the other pieces… the community events, the receptions, the committee things—I’ve been passing more of those off. Which is unusual for me. I used to be able to walk into a room and know exactly how to manage it. Who needs a word, who’s offended by what seating arrangement, all of that nonsense.\n\nLately I just don’t feel as steady with people. And I’ve shortened a few workdays too, which I hate admitting. By the end of the day I’m just not as effective as I normally am. So yes, I’ve pulled back some, even if I keep telling myself it’s only from the nonessential things.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific things that remind you of your cousin's death that you try to avoid thinking about or looking at?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can point to specific things, no. I mean, people keep asking about photographs or places, but it isn’t as tidy as that for me.\n\nThere are moments when something feels… too close, I suppose, but then I’ll get distracted by something completely ordinary, like whether the caterer put the coffee service in the wrong room at a gathering—which, honestly, happens more often than it should—and then I realize I haven’t really answered the question.\n\nSo no, I don’t think there are particular things I’m deliberately avoiding looking at or thinking about. Not that I’ve noticed clearly.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel numb or disconnected from your emotions most of the time since your cousin's death?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… numb might be the word, though I don’t like how dramatic that sounds. It’s more that I know what I’m supposed to feel, and I can perform it well enough.

At the prayer gatherings, for instance, I stood where I was meant to stand, thanked people, held hands, said the right things. And my nieces—sweet girls, they were trying so hard to comfort me—when they hugged me or cried with me, I should have felt… warmth, or relief, or something softening. But inside it was just very quiet. Almost like that affectionate part of me had gone still.

And then of course I found myself noticing ridiculous things, like whether people had left coats on the wrong chairs, which is not the point at all. But yes, I suppose I’ve felt disconnected that way.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.0, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I… I came in because I’m not sleeping right. I’ve always been very disciplined about sleep—early meetings, travel schedules, all of that, you learn to protect it. I used to tell my team sleep was a performance issue, which sounds very corporate, I know.\n\nBut since my cousin died, I lie awake replaying that night. And then if I do sleep, I’m waking up at 3 or 4 in the morning with these little scraps of hospital images in my head. Then I’m in board meetings feeling like I’ve slept in pieces, not a real night. It’s starting to scare me a bit, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know. That’s a hard word. I’ve managed companies through crises, and I’m used to thinking in terms of responsibility and what could have been done differently, but this feels—less clean than that.\n\nI keep turning pieces of it over in my head, but I’m not sure I’d call it guilt exactly. Maybe regret, or just… wishing something had gone differently. I’m sorry, I know that’s not a very direct answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time… not exactly in every setting. If I’m in my office with the door closed, I can usually function. But when I’m driving, it’s like my whole body goes on alert. I’m checking mirrors constantly, watching how fast the trucks are coming up beside me, thinking about where I’d pull off if I had to.\n\nEven on the same road I’ve taken to the office for years, I feel tight, like I’m waiting for something to happen. It’s ridiculous, because I know the route so well—I used to take calls on that drive, plan my day, all of that—but now I’m scanning everything. So yes, I suppose anxious, but it feels more like being braced all the time.\n\nDoctor: Are there things or situations you've been avoiding since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve thought about it that way. Avoiding… maybe not consciously. I’m still going to work, still taking meetings, still doing the normal obligations. I’ve spent so many years being the person who shows up no matter what, you know, flights at dawn, dinners with investors, the whole machinery of it.\n\nBut if you mean specific situations, I… I don’t know. I haven’t made a list in my head. There are things I hesitate around, maybe, but I’m not sure I’d call it avoidance. My assistant did ask if I wanted to move a meeting the other day, and I gave this long explanation about traffic patterns and timing, which probably sounded absurd.\n\nSorry—what exactly do you mean by avoiding? Like places, or conversations, or…?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever experience memories of that night that feel like they're happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Not like I’m literally back there, exactly. I know where I am. But there are these moments—usually very ordinary ones, which is what makes it so strange—when something just cuts through.\n\nA phone ringing late, for instance. I used to be very good about late calls, because in my line of work you learn to take calls at all hours, different time zones and all that. But now if the phone rings at night, I suddenly get this sharp picture of the hospital corridor, the lights, that feeling of standing there waiting.\n\nOr on the highway, if headlights glare in a certain way, I’ll hear the police officer’s voice in my head again. Not a memory I’m choosing to think about—it just arrives. I have to stop for a second, steady myself, remind myself I’m here. So… yes, in that sense.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific places or activities that you've been avoiding since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can name anything cleanly. I mean, I haven’t stopped going to work or anything like that. I still go where I’m supposed to go.\n\nThere are moments where I’ll choose a different route or delay leaving, but then I tell myself it’s because of traffic or because I need to make one more call first. It’s hard to know whether that counts. I’ve spent my whole career optimizing schedules, you know, so it’s very easy for me to make something sound practical when maybe it’s… not entirely practical.\n\nAs for specific places or activities, I’m not sure. Nothing jumps out as an obvious “I refuse to go there” sort of thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding conversations about your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve sat down and decided, “I won’t talk about it.” People don’t bring it up much, honestly. In my world, everyone is very skilled at behaving as though difficult things are logistical complications—send flowers, adjust the calendar, say the appropriate thing, move on.\n\nIf someone asks, I usually give a brief answer. I suppose I do keep it… contained. But I don’t know if that’s avoidance or just privacy. I’ve always been private. Even when my father was ill years ago, I remember I still chaired a board call from the hallway, which sounds terrible now, but at the time it felt like being competent.\n\nSorry, I’m drifting. Conversations about my cousin’s death—I can’t really say I’ve been clearly avoiding them. I just don’t tend to go into it unless someone asks directly.\n\nDoctor: Has your ability to function at work or in your daily life been significantly impacted since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… though I keep wanting to qualify that. I’m still showing up for the essential things—the meetings that really can’t be moved, the decisions people need from me. I’m not just disappearing.\n\nBut I’m not functioning at my usual level. I’ve started handing off more of the community obligations, the receptions and committees and those sorts of things. Normally I can do that kind of room easily—remember everyone’s names, make the right comment, keep things moving. Lately I just don’t feel steady enough with people.\n\nAnd I’ve been shortening some workdays, which I almost never do. I tell myself I’m being efficient, but the truth is I’m not as effective by the end of the day as I should be. So yes, it has had an impact. Not catastrophic, but noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or empty most of the time since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Sad, yes, but it’s a little stranger than that. I don’t know that I would say I’m crying all day or anything so clear.\n\nAt the prayer gatherings, for example, I knew exactly how to behave—stand where I should, thank people, nod, say the right things. And my nieces were very sweet, trying to comfort me. Normally that would reach me, you know? I’m usually… not sentimental exactly, but I can feel affection when it’s there.\n\nLately it’s like I’m doing the proper motions, but there’s no warmth coming back from inside me. No relief. Almost like that part of me has gone quiet. So yes, empty is probably closer than just sad.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it a loss of interest exactly. I mean, I haven’t been going out of my way to do pleasant things, but then I’ve never been someone with a long list of hobbies. Work has always taken up most of the oxygen in the room.\n\nThere are things I usually enjoy—hosting, certain dinners, being involved in events—but lately I’ve treated them more like obligations to be managed. Though some of that may just be timing. There’s always some luncheon or foundation thing, and honestly half the time those are more about who gets seated next to whom than enjoyment.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I’ve noticed I’m less drawn to those things, maybe, but I couldn’t say clearly that I’ve lost interest.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself pulling back from activities that you normally find enjoyable or fulfilling since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose I have. I’m still going to the things that really matter—if there’s a meeting where people genuinely need me, I’m there. I’m not hiding in my bedroom or anything.\n\nBut the other pieces… the community events, the receptions, the committee things—I’ve been passing more of those off. Which is unusual for me. I used to be able to walk into a room and know exactly how to manage it. Who needs a word, who’s offended by what seating arrangement, all of that nonsense.\n\nLately I just don’t feel as steady with people. And I’ve shortened a few workdays too, which I hate admitting. By the end of the day I’m just not as effective as I normally am. So yes, I’ve pulled back some, even if I keep telling myself it’s only from the nonessential things.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific things that remind you of your cousin's death that you try to avoid thinking about or looking at?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can point to specific things, no. I tell myself I’m being efficient, but the truth is I’m not as effective by the end of the day as I should be. So yes, it has had an impact. Not catastrophic, but noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or empty most of the time since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Sad, yes, but it’s a little stranger than that. I don’t know that I would say I’m crying all day or anything so clear.\n\nAt the prayer gatherings, for example, I knew exactly how to behave—stand where I should, thank people, nod, say the right things. And my nieces were very sweet, trying to comfort me. Normally that would reach me, you know? I’m usually… not sentimental exactly, but I can feel affection when it’s there.\n\nLately it’s like I’m doing the proper motions, but there’s no warmth coming back from inside me. No relief. Almost like that part of me has gone quiet. So yes, empty is probably closer than just sad.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it a loss of interest exactly. I mean, I haven’t been going out of my way to do pleasant things, but then I’ve never been someone with a long list of hobbies. Work has always taken up most of the oxygen in the room.\n\nThere are things I usually enjoy—hosting, certain dinners, being involved in events—but lately I’ve treated them more like obligations to be managed. Though some of that may just be timing. There’s always some luncheon or foundation thing, and honestly half the time those are more about who gets seated next to whom than enjoyment.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I’ve noticed I’m less drawn to those things, maybe, but I couldn’t say clearly that I’ve lost interest.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself pulling back from activities that you normally find enjoyable or fulfilling since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose I have. I’m still going to the things that really matter—if there’s a meeting where people genuinely need me, I’m there. I’m not hiding in my bedroom or anything.\n\nBut the other pieces… the community events, the receptions, the committee things—I’ve been passing more of those off. Which is unusual for me. I used to be able to walk into a room and know exactly how to manage it. Who needs a word, who’s offended by what seating arrangement, all of that nonsense.\n\nLately I just don’t feel as steady with people. And I’ve shortened a few workdays too, which I hate admitting. By the end of the day I’m just not as effective as I normally am. So yes, I’ve pulled back some, even if I keep telling myself it’s only from the nonessential things.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific things that remind you of your cousin's death that you try to avoid thinking about or looking at?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can point to specific things, no. I mean, people keep asking about photographs or places, but it isn’t as tidy as that for me.\n\nThere are moments when something feels… too close, I suppose, but then I’ll get distracted by something completely ordinary, like whether the caterer put the coffee service in the wrong room at a gathering—which, honestly, happens more often than it should—and then I realize I haven’t really answered the question.\n\nSo no, I don’t think there are particular things I’m deliberately avoiding looking at or thinking about. Not that I’ve noticed clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel numb or disconnected from your emotions most of the time since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… numb might be the word, though I don’t like how dramatic that sounds. It’s more that I know what I’m supposed to feel, and I can perform it well enough.\n\nAt the prayer gatherings, for instance, I stood where I was meant to stand, thanked people, held hands, said the right things. And my nieces—sweet girls, they were trying so hard to comfort me—when they hugged me or cried with me, I should have felt… warmth, or relief, or something softening. But inside it was just very quiet. Almost like that affectionate part of me had gone still.\n\nAnd then of course I found myself noticing ridiculous things, like whether people had left coats on the wrong chairs, which is not the point at all. But yes, I suppose I’ve felt disconnected that way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I’d call them flashbacks exactly. That sounds like… a movie playing, doesn’t it? It isn’t quite that dramatic.

But yes, there are these sharp moments. If the phone rings late, or if I’m driving and headlights hit the windshield a certain way on the highway, suddenly I get this picture in my head of the hospital corridor. Or I hear the police officer’s voice again—well, not hear it out loud, but it’s just there. Very clear.

And then I have to stop for a second and steady myself. Usually I can keep going. I’ve always been good at keeping going—too good, probably. My staff would tell you I can take a call in the middle of a crisis and still ask whether the agenda was printed correctly. But yes… those moments do push in on me.

I’ve started handing off more of the community obligations, the receptions and committees and those sorts of things. Lately I just don’t feel steady enough with people.\n\nAnd I’ve been shortening some workdays, which I almost never do. I tell myself I’m being efficient, but the truth is I’m not as effective by the end of the day as I should be. So yes, it has had an impact. I’m usually… not sentimental exactly, but I can feel affection when it’s there.\n\nLately it’s like I’m doing the proper motions, but there’s no warmth coming back from inside me. No relief. I mean, I haven’t been going out of my way to do pleasant things, but then I’ve never been someone with a long list of hobbies. Work has always taken up most of the oxygen in the room.\n\nThere are things I usually enjoy—hosting, certain dinners, being involved in events—but lately I’ve treated them more like obligations to be managed. There’s always some luncheon or foundation thing, and honestly half the time those are more about who gets seated next to whom than enjoyment.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think so. Not entirely, maybe, because “no longer able” sounds very final. But since it happened, when there should be comfort or something good, it doesn’t really reach me.

At the prayer gatherings, I knew exactly how to behave—thank people, stand with the family, accept condolences. And my nieces were so sweet, trying to comfort me, putting their arms around me. I remember thinking I should feel warmth from that, or relief, or gratitude in the normal way. Instead I just felt… properly arranged on the outside, and quiet inside.

It’s odd, because I can still notice details—who brought what, whether someone forgot to put out enough cups, all those ridiculous little things—but the affectionate part, the part that should soften when people are kind, feels like it’s gone very still.
