But now if the phone rings at night, I suddenly get this sharp picture of the hospital corridor, the lights, that feeling of standing there waiting.\n\nOr on the highway, if headlights glare in a certain way, I’ll hear the police officer’s voice in my head again. Not a memory I’m choosing to think about—it just arrives. I have to stop for a second, steady myself, remind myself I’m here. So… yes, in that sense.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific places or activities that you've been avoiding since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can name anything cleanly. I mean, I haven’t stopped going to work or anything like that. I still go where I’m supposed to go.\n\nThere are moments where I’ll choose a different route or delay leaving, but then I tell myself it’s because of traffic or because I need to make one more call first. It’s hard to know whether that counts. I’ve spent my whole career optimizing schedules, you know, so it’s very easy for me to make something sound practical when maybe it’s… not entirely practical.\n\nAs for specific places or activities, I’m not sure. Nothing jumps out as an obvious “I refuse to go there” sort of thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding conversations about your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve sat down and decided, “I won’t talk about it.” People don’t bring it up much, honestly. In my world, everyone is very skilled at behaving as though difficult things are logistical complications—send flowers, adjust the calendar, say the appropriate thing, move on.\n\nIf someone asks, I usually give a brief answer. I suppose I do keep it… contained. But I don’t know if that’s avoidance or just privacy. I’ve always been private. Even when my father was ill years ago, I remember I still chaired a board call from the hallway, which sounds terrible now, but at the time it felt like being competent.\n\nSorry, I’m drifting. Conversations about my cousin’s death—I can’t really say I’ve been clearly avoiding them. I just don’t tend to go into it unless someone asks directly.\n\nDoctor: Has your ability to function at work or in your daily life been significantly impacted since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… though I keep wanting to qualify that. I’m still showing up for the essential things—the meetings that really can’t be moved, the decisions people need from me. I’m not just disappearing.\n\nBut I’m not functioning at my usual level. I’ve started handing off more of the community obligations, the receptions and committees and those sorts of things. Normally I can do that kind of room easily—remember everyone’s names, make the right comment, keep things moving. Lately I just don’t feel steady enough with people.\n\nAnd I’ve been shortening some workdays, which I almost never do. I tell myself I’m being efficient, but the truth is I’m not as effective by the end of the day as I should be. So yes, it has had an impact. Not catastrophic, but noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or empty most of the time since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Sad, yes, but it’s a little stranger than that. I don’t know that I would say I’m crying all day or anything so clear.\n\nAt the prayer gatherings, for example, I knew exactly how to behave—stand where I should, thank people, nod, say the right things. And my nieces were very sweet, trying to comfort me. Normally that would reach me, you know? I’m usually… not sentimental exactly, but I can feel affection when it’s there.\n\nLately it’s like I’m doing the proper motions, but there’s no warmth coming back from inside me. No relief. Almost like that part of me has gone quiet. So yes, empty is probably closer than just sad.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it a loss of interest exactly. I mean, I haven’t been going out of my way to do pleasant things, but then I’ve never been someone with a long list of hobbies. Work has always taken up most of the oxygen in the room.\n\nThere are things I usually enjoy—hosting, certain dinners, being involved in events—but lately I’ve treated them more like obligations to be managed. Though some of that may just be timing. There’s always some luncheon or foundation thing, and honestly half the time those are more about who gets seated next to whom than enjoyment.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I’ve noticed I’m less drawn to those things, maybe, but I couldn’t say clearly that I’ve lost interest.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself pulling back from activities that you normally find enjoyable or fulfilling since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose I have. I’m still going to the things that really matter—if there’s a meeting where people genuinely need me, I’m there. I’m not hiding in my bedroom or anything.\n\nBut the other pieces… the community events, the receptions, the committee things—I’ve been passing more of those off. Which is unusual for me. I used to be able to walk into a room and know exactly how to manage it. Who needs a word, who’s offended by what seating arrangement, all of that nonsense.\n\nLately I just don’t feel as steady with people. And I’ve shortened a few workdays too, which I hate admitting. By the end of the day I’m just not as effective as I normally am. So yes, I’ve pulled back some, even if I keep telling myself it’s only from the nonessential things.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific things that remind you of your cousin's death that you try to avoid thinking about or looking at?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can point to specific things, no. I mean, people keep asking about photographs or places, but it isn’t as tidy as that for me.\n\nThere are moments when something feels… too close, I suppose, but then I’ll get distracted by something completely ordinary, like whether the caterer put the coffee service in the wrong room at a gathering—which, honestly, happens more often than it should—and then I realize I haven’t really answered the question.\n\nSo no, I don’t think there are particular things I’m deliberately avoiding looking at or thinking about. Not that I’ve noticed clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel numb or disconnected from your emotions most of the time since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… numb might be the word, though I don’t like how dramatic that sounds. It’s more that I know what I’m supposed to feel, and I can perform it well enough.\n\nAt the prayer gatherings, for instance, I stood where I was meant to stand, thanked people, held hands, said the right things. And my nieces—sweet girls, they were trying so hard to comfort me—when they hugged me or cried with me, I should have felt… warmth, or relief, or something softening. But inside it was just very quiet. Almost like that affectionate part of me had gone still.\n\nAnd then of course I found myself noticing ridiculous things, like whether people had left coats on the wrong chairs, which is not the point at all. But yes, I suppose I’ve felt disconnected that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any flashbacks of the night your cousin died?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call them flashbacks exactly. That sounds like… a movie playing, doesn’t it? It isn’t quite that dramatic.\n\nBut yes, there are these sharp moments. If the phone rings late, or if I’m driving and headlights hit the windshield a certain way on the highway, suddenly I get this picture in my head of the hospital corridor. Or I hear the police officer’s voice again—well, not hear it out loud, but it’s just there. Very clear.\n\nAnd then I have to stop for a second and steady myself. Usually I can keep going. I’ve always been good at keeping going—too good, probably. My staff would tell you I can take a call in the middle of a crisis and still ask whether the agenda was printed correctly. But yes… those moments do push in on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're no longer able to experience positive emotions since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Not entirely, maybe, because “no longer able” sounds very final. But since it happened, when there should be comfort or something good, it doesn’t really reach me.\n\nAt the prayer gatherings, I knew exactly how to behave—thank people, stand with the family, accept condolences. And my nieces were so sweet, trying to comfort me, putting their arms around me. I remember thinking I should feel warmth from that, or relief, or gratitude in the normal way. Instead I just felt… properly arranged on the outside, and quiet inside.\n\nIt’s odd, because I can still notice details—who brought what, whether someone forgot to put out enough cups, all those ridiculous little things—but the affectionate part, the part that should soften when people are kind, feels like it’s gone very still.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
